backend/data/book/The_Four_Sacred_Secrets_structure.json:99:          "text": "are? How did we become such self-preoccupied individuals?\nWhy Am I Unhappy?\nWho is the \u0000rst person you wake up to every morning? Who is the person you\nspend every moment of your life with, even in your dreams? Who is it you are\nwith when you are alone or when you keep company?\nYou.\nDo you love that you? Are you caring to that you? Or are you critical and\njudgmental of that you? Are you your own best friend?\nThink about what happens when you are angry with a friend, a family\nmember, or a colleague. You try to change them. You give them advice or you tell\nthem you won’t stand for their behavior. You may even pray for them to change.\nIf they refuse, you may distance yourself from them. You make fewer plans with\nthem, and you are slower to return their phone calls. If things become\nunbearable, you might even sever the relationship altogether.\nBut what if the person you are dissatis\u0000ed with is yourself?\nWhat if the person you do not like is yourself?\nWhat if the person you absolutely hate is you?\nPlease pause here. Inhale deeply and slowly exhale a couple of times. Quietly\nshine the spotlight of attention on your relationship with yourself. Bring your\nattention to moments when you felt a beautiful state of care and respect for\nyourself. Breathe deeply and stay in a state of observation of those moments.\nNow bring your attention to moments when you were in suffering states of\ndissatisfaction or dislike for yourself. Breathe deeply and observe your state in\nthose moments.\nHopefully, you have had some moments when you felt a beautiful\nconnection to yourself. You simply loved yourself as you are.\nThere have likely also been times when you felt pain or discomfort within. In\nsuch moments you may have either sought out external solutions and escapes or\naccepted the inner war as “normal,” forgetting that you are not this warring self;\nyou are a beautiful self. You are not a sheep fearing your survival in the jungle;\nyou are a lion.\nIf we don’t have a beautiful relationship with ourselves, everything about us\n—th e way we walk, the way we talk, our speech, our way of thinking, and our\ne\u0000orts at success—will be tainted with a nagging sense of self-doubt. What can\nwe possibly achieve when we are stuck in this su\u0000ering state? We need to dissolve\nit.\nUnable to cope with the ever-widening internal divide, many people\ndesperately try to chisel and re-chisel their bodies. Millions take to narcotics,\ndrink alcohol, or even contemplate or commit suicide.\nAnd yet, for all our grasping at external solutions to changing how we feel\nabout ourselves, there is no evidence that any human being has ever found\nhappiness while at war with oneself. After all, if you spend the majority of your\ntime in this warring state, how much energy will you have left to enjoy your\nrelationships, wealth, leisure, or success?\nWhat else happens when we are not at peace with ourselves?\nDid you ever play tag as a child? The way I played the game was to stand in a\ncircle and say, “Eeny meeny miney mo, catch a tiger by the toe . . . ,” then\neliminate one kid after the other until the last one left was the chaser.\nWhat were we doing? Instead of making a clear choice, we allowed luck to\ndecide. No one needed to take responsibility for the selection of the chaser.\nIf we are not harmonious within ourselves, quite often that is how we\napproach the most important decisions of our lives, even as adults. We are\nindecisive because our su\u0000ering states take away faith and respect for ourselves\nand our decisions. We continue to play “eeny meeny miney mo” in the process of\nselecting our jobs, our spouses, or our business partners. We simply cannot\ncon\u0000dently decide or choose.\nEven if we do choose, we continue to doubt. We doubt that we are in love\nwith the right person, sometimes even after three years of being in a relationship.\nWe doubt our choice of career a decade into the job. We doubt our course of\nstudy in college long after graduation. When we are lost in the clash of\ncon\u0000icting views and opinions, we forget that life can be beautiful indeed.\nUnable to bear this inner chaos, we run from one quick \u0000x to the next,\nlooking for anything to silence the con\u0000icting and nagging voices that create this\ninner claustrophobia. And when nothing leads to any lasting change, it can seem\nas if the world has given us a raw deal.\n“But I’m a good person!” we cry. “I have never harmed anyone in my life. So\nwhy am I so unhappy?”\nThree Expressions of the Warring Self\nIn the famous epic Indian poem Ramayana, the villain, King Ravana, has a\nunique dilemma. He was not a foolish or evil king, like so many of the villains\nwe’ve come to know. Ravana was a great scholar. He was well versed in the\nscriptures, and he created great prosperity for his kingdom.\nSo how did the actions of this man, considered virtuous in every way, lead to\nthe death of his brother, his son, and his entire clan? What led him to kidnap the\nwife of the hero, Rama—an action that led to Ravana’s entire kingdom going\ndown in \u0000ames? How did such an erudite man become so destructive?\nThe story describes Ravana as a man with ten heads. His many heads were\nsymbolic of his dissenting values and obsessive desires that kept him trapped in\nhis own head. None of his knowledge was any help when it came to silencing the\ntorment of his con\u0000icting desires and values. This was a man at war with\nhimself, and it did not take long for that inner war to infect everyone around\nhim.\nWhen you read the story of Ravana, you might \u0000nd yourself asking a\nquestion that is as relevant today as it was in ancient times:\nWhy do good people become bad?\nHave we not all asked this question at some point in our lives? We look at a\nsister, son, or friend who has lost their way and we wonder: What on earth went\nwrong? We look at the leader or the artist we once believed in and ask ourselves:\nHow did they go so far oﬀ track?\nWhen a person is lost in a state of inner war, they become like Ravana. Not\nonly are they self-destructive, but they have the power to destroy others. A\nperson could be the most well-intentioned in a crowd of hundreds, but if their\ninner self is a battleground of con\u0000icting values, they will bring chaos to their\nworld.\nThe \u0000re of our inner war can be sparked by con\u0000icting desires, such as:\nI want to be a selﬂess mother, but then I’ll have to give up my career. . . . I can’t\nhave it all.\nI want that promotion, but then I’ll never be able to travel the world. . . . I guess\nI have to settle.\nI want a relationship, but I don’t want to miss out on the single life. . . . I’m\ngoing to be unhappy no matter what.\nOur inner battle could also be because of the con\u0000ict between our ideals and\nour actualities. We yearn to be virtuous, but are drawn to vice. We yearn to be\npatient and kind, but are \u0000lled with anger and intolerance.\nBut when you have no way of freeing yourself from this kind of inner\ncon\u0000ict, it does not matter which path you take. Your dissatisfaction can\nintensify into states of depression and even a hatred of yourself and your world.\nThat is exactly what happened to Ravana. Even though he knew his\ncon\u0000icting desires would be his undoing, and the fate of his kingdom hung in\nthe balance, he could not resist.\nSo many of us live our lives with the same inner torment as Ravana. But if\nour inner world is a battleground, how can we ever know the beautiful states of\nhappiness or freedom?\n“Battleground” might seem like a strong word. Sure, we all feel unsatis\u0000ed\nand underappreciated a lot of the time. But that’s life.\nOr is it?\nWhile the source of the struggles in our lives may appear to be external, in\nfact it is we who are unleashing a destructive force into the world by embracing\none of the following expressions of the warring self:\nThe \u0000rst expression of our inner war is a “shrinking self.”\nThe Shrinking Self\nWhen Alex was around twelve or thirteen years old, he was bullied because he\nwas frail and much smaller than his classmates. To cope with his state of\nhumiliation, he went on to become a gymnast. By the time he reached college, he\nwas one of the most handsome students on campus: girls virtually swooned over\nhim.\nAnd his success did not stop there. He made it big in business, became\nwealthy, and married a beautiful woman.\nYet, to this day, Alex feels highly self-conscious and inadequate because he\ncan’t resist the desire to compare himself with others. He often feels unsure if his\nwife feels or needs his love because he compares her behavior with that of\nwomen from his past.\nHe had thought that if he rose to the very top in every \u0000eld he pursued, he\nwould have no reason to feel inferior to anyone else. He had decided as a child\nthat he would get to a place where others would compare themselves with him—\ninstead of the other way around.\nBut after embarking on an inner journey of truth, he saw that reality was very\ndi\u0000erent. His obsessive habit of frequent comparison had not ceased despite all\nthe success and wealth he had created!\nThis entanglement in su\u0000ering states led to a strange pattern in his business.\nEvery deal he made, even if it resulted in a good pro\u0000t, wasn’t good enough. He\nbelieved that others always fared better. He also felt that success came to him\nonly after immense hard work. Everyone else, in his mind, had a much easier\nride.\nThis pattern changed only when his inner disharmony dissolved. A new\nintelligence arose in him during a mystic meditation with Krishnaji, in which he\nwoke up from the illusion of a limited self and experienced a state of\nconsciousness in which he no longer felt division or the need to compare.\nAfter Alex experienced that transformation in consciousness, he found it\nmuch easier to access beautiful states of creativity and a clear mind—and it’s\nfrom this place that he now builds his achievements.\nThis shrinking self that Alex experienced is not uncommon. Oftentimes it\nmanifests as debilitating self-consciousness, di\u0000dence, or low self-esteem.\nSometimes, as in the case of Alex, our desperate attempts at enlarging our\nshrinking self can make us extremely aggressive.\nBut what is the truth of the shrinking self?\nThe shrinking self is fueled by the addictive habit of comparing ourselves to\nothers and feeling that we do not match up.\nWe feel small and insigni\u0000cant. We are ill at ease in the presence of those who\nwe believe are more intelligent, more beautiful, and more talented. We imagine\nothers judge us as inferior, and we become self-conscious.\nA shrinking self creates uncertainty and makes us step back from the joys of\nlife. We lack the courage to go after what our heart yearns for.\nThe Destructive Self\nThe second expression of our inner war is a “destructive self.”\nAlicia and Greg, a couple from Switzerland, had been having problems for\nover a decade, but they stayed together for their only daughter. Finally, when\ntheir daughter went away to college, they decided to go ahead with a divorce.\nUnfortunately, their problems did not end there.\nGreg had always been a reasonable man, but once the divorce was \u0000nalized,\nhe changed. He gave in to hate. He made it his mission to make Alicia’s life\ndi\u0000cult. In the years they were married, she had been loud and dominant, while\nhe had been submissive. It was as if the separation had freed him up to indulge in\nyears of pent-up anger and aggression. He constantly compared his disturbing\nand broken family life with how it could have been and blamed his wife for his\nunhappiness.\nGreg was well educated and \u0000nancially independent—he had the means to\nlive a peaceful life—but instead he became obsessed with settling scores.\nWhen we give in to the state of the destructive self, we become emotionally\nimbalanced, impulsive, and volatile. A destructive self can manifest as\nperfectionism, excessive ambition, and ruthlessness—or as an addiction to\npleasure, unwholesome habits, or work.\nFrom this state, we perceive others to be competitors or enemies. Dominating\nthem or displaying our power becomes more important to us than our own\ngrowth and well-being. We make adversaries out of friends and family. We\nbecome hardened and insensitive—and we \u0000nd very few people to call our own.\nIt makes us form unhealthy relationships.\nDriving the destructive self is the addictive habit of comparing our lives with\nwhat is and what should have been.\nBut not only do we compare—w e also hold someone else responsible for our\nvexing reality.\nLife becomes a war.\nThe Inert Self\nWe call the third expression of our inner war an “inert self.”\nFor her entire life, Beth compared herself with her siblings, and she always\ncame up short. She was considered less attractive than her sisters, and a learning\ndisability prevented her from achieving the same professional success they\nenjoyed. Making matters worse, her parents taunted her for being lazy, which\nonly aggravated her inner war.\nAnd so she fell into a vicious cycle: she ate more, she did not exercise or work,\nand she lost the little con\u0000dence she’d once had. She squandered whatever\nmoney she had by making foolish investments. She fantasized about the life she\nknew she deserved but that seemed hopelessly out of reach.\nThe predominant characteristics of an inert self are indi\u0000erence, lack of\nresponsibility, laziness, and procrastination. We lack drive and motivation. The\none activity that tempts us is daydreaming! What drives the experience of the\ninert self? Once again, it’s the habit of comparing ourselves with others—but\nwhereas the shrinking self struggles with comparison and experiences life as a\nkind of endless race, the inert self gives up without e\u0000ort.\nWe give up hope that anything good will come our way.\nIf we happen to \u0000nd ourselves in any of these stories, we may be tempted to\ninstantly write them o\u0000 as bad habits or behavioral traits that need to be \u0000xed.\nIn yearning to break free of the shrinking self, we may get into adventure\nsports. We assume that the solution to low self-esteem is to project a glorious\nself-image—but that only unconsciously furthers our inner divide.\nIn an attempt to transcend the destructive self, we try to re\u0000ne our behavior,\nour culture, or our language. We try to manage and tame ourselves, but often\nend up merely trading in one addiction for another.\nFinally, to break free of the state of inertia, we make desperate e\u0000orts to drag\nourselves to the gym, to detox our bodies, or even cleanse our livers, without ever\naddressing our consciousness.\nAre these lasting solutions? How could there be true transformation without\n\u0000rst bringing attention to the truth that these are but symptoms of a deeper\ninternal war?\nPlease pause here. Breathe deeply. Observe moments of dislike, dissatisfaction, or\nhate for yourself. What self has your inner battle morphed into?\nPassively observe the impact of these selves on your life.\nSearching for Lost Love\nThe great Mughal emperor Akbar was known for indulging in intellectually\nstimulating challenges and debates with his ministers. The story goes that he\nonce threw a rather funny challenge at his ministers:\nHe asked them to \u0000nd the biggest fool in the kingdom.\nBirbal, a particularly witty minister, spent all day searching high and low but\ncame up empty-handed when it came to producing this great fool. By dusk, a\nfatigued Birbal was making his return to the emperor, when his eyes fell on a\nman searching for something under a dim lamppost.\nBirbal approached the man and asked what he was looking for.\nAt the sight of the minister in royal robes, the old man respectfully said that\nhe was searching for his lost key. Filled with sympathy, Birbal joined the man in\nhis search. After a few minutes Birbal asked the man where exactly he had lost\nthe key. The man pointed toward a dark corner very far away from the spot\nwhere he had been searching.\n“If you lost the key over there, why are you searching for it here?”\n“Because here there is light.”\nBirbal smiled, con\u0000dent he had completed the task successfully. He went\nback to the emperor and presented the fool and won his reward.\nHow often do we wonder why none of our external solutions bring peace to\nthe war raging within? Sure, the world abounds with quick \u0000xes that bring on\ntemporary “feel-good” states. But when the next challenge hits, we are\ncatapulted back into the same whirl of con\u0000ict, condemnation, and self-\nloathing.\nLike the old man in Birbal’s story, we cannot solve our problems, because we\ndon’t know where to look for the solutions. Even when we are willing to look\nwithin, we seek to cut through our su\u0000ering states with a sword. We blame\nourselves for our reserve, our de\u0000ated self-worth, or our self-doubt. But what if\nthey are mere manifestations of something deeper?\nAt the root of all unhappiness is an incessant obsession with the self.\nWhen we share this profound and life-transforming realization with people,\nwe are often met with immediate resistance.\nBut it is my partner who cheated . . .\nIt’s my children who don’t listen . . .\nIt’s my boss who takes credit for my work . . .\nSurely there must be exceptions to the idea that “all unhappiness comes from\nself-obsession,” we may think—especially since we are the ones reading a book\non transforming our lives! The one who stays late when others leave \u0000ve minutes\nearly. The one who makes sure the kids are fed, the plumber is paid, the dentist\nappointments are made—and on and on and on . . .\nHow could anyone who works so hard to be a good person possibly be\ncausing their own stress? Surely there is some mistake.\nHow could anyone so selﬂess be self-obsessed?\nFirst of all, let us clarify that there is a clear di\u0000erentiation between sel\u0000shness\nand self-obsession. We are not talking about acting without regard for others.\nWhat we mean by self-obsession is an internal preoccupation with self.\nNow, before we deny our own self-obsession, let us ask ourselves: How often\ndo we wage imaginary wars in our minds, measuring ourselves against others and\nobsessing about what they think about us? How many times have we posted\nsomething on Facebook, imagined a negative reaction, and posted a reactive\nstatement before anyone actually responded?\nAnd how many times have we simply blamed someone else for the way we are\nfeeling, even though it is our own thoughts spinning round and round in a cycle\nof self-obsession?\nLet us not forget that this state of self-preoccupation is the breeding ground\nof all unhappiness and inner war.\nUntil we make peace with ourselves, our inner worlds will be battlegrounds\nof con\u0000ict:\nWhy is that person more loved than I am?\nWhy can’t my life be more like hers?\nHe is so attractive and witty. Why not me?\nWhy was I not born with a silver spoon in my mouth? Why him?\nYou would continue to sing, Why me? Or, Why not me?\nOur inner struggle, however, has nothing to do with external situations. That\nyou are not as tall as your father or as successful as your college roommate—\nthese are facts of life, neither good nor bad. Such facts might cause\ninconvenience or di\u0000culty, but there are always solutions to be found to such\nexternal problems.\nNow, of course, we are not denying the challenges of human life that we all\nexperience to a varying degree. We only have so much time in these bodies. We\nare not all granted perfect health or loving families. For many people on this\nearth, life is neither easy nor gentle.\nBut when we toss a maelstrom of self-obsession on top of life’s hardships, we\nbecome \u0000xated on the unfairness of life. Our mind makes everything—our\nbodies, our lives, our world—appear incomplete or ugly. We begin to su\u0000er from\nan exaggerated and painful sense of “injustice,” as if the universe purposely\ndenied us everything. We complain, “How can we possibly know peace with the\nlot we’ve been given?”\nAnd have you not noticed that, in such su\u0000ering states, problems and chaos\nmount in our lives?\nWhen self-obsession takes over, the actual issues of life will remain\nunaddressed. We develop a perpetually insecure mind that easily perceives insult\nor disrespect even where there is none. We grow discontented with who we are\nand become obsessed with an image of who we should be. In a desperate attempt\nat psychological survival, we try to conform to others’ standards and win their\nattention.\nWe become people who wear many masks, while the true power of the\nbeautiful state of consciousness eludes us.\nWe may engage in conversations on self-love and self-care, but too often we\nget wrapped up in super\u0000cial \u0000xes that do nothing to address the core problem\nof obsessive self-preoccupation.\nAnd how complete is our self-care while we allow our inner selves to linger in\nsu\u0000ering states and stay wounded and sore? How authentic is our self-love when\nwe take a beautiful vacation but do nothing to take a break from the incessant\ninner chatter? For in such a state we are lost and disconnected. It is impossible to\ncelebrate life.\nAuthentic self-love requires us to step away from self-obsession and step into\na beautiful state.\nHow?\nBy moving out of painful obsession and into gentle observation.\nAccording to Dr. Daniel J. Siegel, a clinical professor of psychiatry, every time\nwe move into a state of observation, the neural activity shifts in our brains from\nthe amygdala, the fear and anger center, to the mid-prefrontal region of\nintelligent thought and an expanded sense of connection.\nIf this is a scientist’s perception of observation, a mystic’s perception of the\nfruit of observation would be the activation of the third eye as depicted in the\nimages of many Eastern dieties.\nOur journey to a beautiful self begins with truth, for truth alone can set us\nfree. If we can see the truth of our own inner war and the way it has warped our\nperception of life, we can transform it without judging it. At that point, a\nbeautiful state of calm descends upon us. If we embrace our incessant\ncomparison with others and the depth of our disconnected state, without\n\u0000ghting with it or feeling ashamed of it, the beautiful state of connection would\nspontaneously arise within us. If we can witness the absolute chaos in our lives\nthat emerges from our incessant dissatisfaction with ourselves, a new order will\nbegin to emerge.\nLet us share with you the story of Maureen, a Mediterranean woman who\ncourageously transformed her sense of self.\nWhen we met Maureen, she was in her early forties. She worked in the\ncorporate world, and she projected a tough exterior. She was athletic and didn’t\nsmile much. She said even the most ordinary words with a certain sense of\nsti\u0000ness. But as I led all the seekers into a journey of wholeness during a retreat,\nMaureen experienced a true metamorphosis.\nShe was a child of only eight or nine when she was raped by a stranger. As he\nwalked away he spat on her and said, “You are an ugly-looking girl.”\nShe had seen many therapists over the years to get over her rage and the\nhaunting feeling of disrespect for herself. She had been married twice. She held a\nmajor portfolio and was notorious for her e\u0000ciency and ruthlessness, but she\nnever felt the respect she desired.\nAs we led her into a deep meditative state, for the \u0000rst time she observed this\nentire event as a passive witness. There was no warring self, screaming at her that\n“this should not have happened. My life should have been something else.”\nThere was no “should have been” or “should not be.” That event simply was.\nEvery event of her life simply was. This was the \u0000rst time she saw her life without\nbeing engrossed in her wounded self.\nAs she sank deeper into the Limitless Field meditation, she had a very\nuncommon experience. She felt as if she were embraced by the universe itself. It\nwas as if the universe were a living being that wanted to hold her and help her\nheal this deeply wounded part of herself.\nShe told us it was as if her heart were broken glass, and in that transcendental\nembrace it was being pieced together for the \u0000rst time.\nBut while that unearthly experience was deeply powerful, the way it changed\nher life was just as miraculous.\nAn experience that had always seemed so painfully de\u0000ning, so scarring, had\nbecome just another moment of her life, one she could look back at from a\nbeautiful state of calm.\nSince her powerfully transformative experience, she has experienced new\ndepths of love and compassion for herself. The thrust toward self-obsession has\nceased. When she was o\u0000ered the latest in a long line of promotions, for the \u0000rst\ntime she said no. She decided to further her inward journey, using what she had\ndiscovered in the most sel\u0000ess way possible.\n“I want to take this time to heal others,” she said.\nMaureen has since rede\u0000ned her role as a mentor to the young entry-level\nemployees at her organization. She is at peace with the love she has found in her\nlife.\nWe do not need to have had such trauma in our past to understand\nMaureen’s pain. But every one of us must become free of memories that haunt\nus like bad dreams. We must awaken to a state of harmony.\nIf we observe the nature of all the warring-self states, we see that they are all\ninner critics tearing us apart, stealing the beautiful states of joy and calm from\nour lives. No matter what our personal histories may be, whether they are simple\nor complicated, when we are caught in the grip of these warring selves, we\ncriticize everything about ourselves: our appearance, our status, our home and\nfamily, our life itself. At the root of our inner divide is the habit of incessant\ncommentary, which divides every experience of our lives into “should be” and\n“should not be.” It is this habit that drives us to comparison and inner war.\nWhen you look at your body, you do not see it as it is; you comment on every\ninch of it as it should be or should not be. When you are with your family, you are\nnot present to it as it is; you comment on every member as should be this way or\nshould not be this way. When you enter your home, you do not relish it; you\ncomment on it as should have been bigger or smaller or should not have been.\nWhen you get to work, you are not \u0000lled with a sense of purpose or creativity;\nyou judge every day as I should have been elsewhere or I should not have been here.\nWhen you emerge into being an observer of life, all commentaries become\nredundant and fall away from you like dry leaves. They get carried away in the\nriver of awareness. A deep sense of calm and joy radiates from your being. In this\nmagni\u0000cent state of consciousness every failure is assimilated without the need\nto blame oneself or another. Every defeat is accepted without the need to justify\noneself or condemn another. Other people’s words and statements do not\nbecome your way of looking at yourself or your body. You are comfortable with\nyour angry self, your jealous self, and your lonely self. There is no part of you\nthat you make wrong. You are at ease with the entirety of yourself. In this state\nof consciousness from which observation proceeds, you realize the true meaning\nof compassion and freedom.\nIn the absence of our incessant commentaries on every experience of life as\neither good or bad, ugly or beautiful, should be or should not be, we go beyond\npride and humiliation, beyond guilt and regret. We enter the realm of pure\nconsciousness wherein all is sacred. All simply is. Every person in your life simply\nis. Life simply is—a \u0000ow of this universe.\nAnd when we emerge from this inner war, we will awaken to our heart’s\npassions and the greater purpose of our lives. We are more present for our loved\nones and more able to give to our community and the world. We are truly\ninspired to create a di\u0000erence for the people around us.\nWe have moved beyond the “should be” and the “should not be” to the\n“what is.” We are in love with life. We are in love with ourselves. It is a beautiful\nstate of consciousness.\nPlease pause here. Slow down. Breathe and feel your body. There is no “should be”\nor “should not be.” Your body simply is.\nBreathe slowly and hold your family in your heart. There is no “should be” or\n“should not be.” This is your family. It simply is.\nBreathe deeply. Look at your home. There is no “should be” or “should not be.”\nThis is your home. It simply is.\nFinally, observe your own self-criticism with kindness. Don’t be angry with\nyourself for being judgmental. Smile at it. There is no “should be” or “should not\nbe.” There is only what is.\nA serene observation of what is leads you to the beautiful states of calm and\ninner wholeness. As your war with yourself ends, you sing a new song: Mine is a\nbeautiful life.",
backend/data/book/The_Four_Sacred_Secrets_structure.json:113:      "text": "The Second Life Journey: Dissolve\nthe Inner Divide\nBy Preethaji\nWhen our daughter, Lokaa, was \u0000ve years old, her English tutor gave her a poem\ntitled “Inside-Outside,” by Abigail Gri\u0000th, that began:\nMy inside self and my outside self\nAre diﬀerent as can be\nThe poem went on to describe all the ways in which the character’s physical\nappearance was at odds with the way she felt about herself.\nThe tutor then asked Lokaa to write her own version of the sample poem.\nTwenty-\u0000ve minutes later, with Lokaa no closer to writing a poem like the\nsample, the tutor handed her poem to me and said, “Lokaa’s poem is not like the\nsample poem; you will nevertheless like what she has written anyway,” and\nwalked away.\nHere’s what Lokaa wrote:\nI am pretty\nas a buzzing bee\nI don’t want to be anyone\ndiﬀerent than me.\nI’m smart and intelligent,\nSweet and kind,\nHow should I change\nwhat is already ﬁne?\nI want to be a friend\nto all who are good\nI am me\nand you are you!\nThe model poem portrayed an individual with two inner voices, while Lokaa\ndid not have that inner divide. When people met Lokaa, they witnessed someone\nin a beautiful state—but that experience was not unique to her childhood. She\ncontinues to have that same inner clarity and non-con\u0000icted state of being even\ntoday.\nPerhaps many of us were like this as children. We have all hugged a loved one\nwholly and unselfconsciously. We’ve delighted in the feel of a dog’s fur or the\ntaste of fruit. We’ve contemplated the rainbows the sun makes in a water\ndroplet. The simple pleasures of human life were a wonder to our senses. We felt\njoyful, complete, whole.\nSomewhere along the line, many of us became divided, con\u0000icted individuals\nand self-obsession set in. In many ways our society accentuates this feeling of\ndivision. For those of us educated in a system based on test scores and rankings,\nwe learned to compare, compete, and judge. We were taught to see peers not as\nfriends but as competitors.\nThe battle we were \u0000ghting was not just against other people. We were also\ntaught to wage war with ourselves. We began to paint a picture in our minds of\nthe person we wanted to be and we would become frustrated when we fell short\nof our expectations. We unconsciously became either pleasers or provers. As\npleasers, we live in fear of others being displeased with us and therefore make our\nchoices and actions to appease them. As provers, we remember our past hurts,\nhold angry conversations in our minds, and make our life choices to prove the\npeople who hurt us wrong. We become so accustomed to life during wartime\nthat we forget there is another way to be.\nBut how is it that we became so disconnected from our beautiful state of\nwholeness in the \u0000rst place? How did we cease to be the beautiful self we truly\nare? How did we become such self-preoccupied individuals?\nWhy Am I Unhappy?\nWho is the \u0000rst person you wake up to every morning? Who is the person you\nspend every moment of your life with, even in your dreams? Who is it you are\nwith when you are alone or when you keep company?\nYou.\nDo you love that you? Are you caring to that you? Or are you critical and\njudgmental of that you? Are you your own best friend?\nThink about what happens when you are angry with a friend, a family\nmember, or a colleague. You try to change them. You give them advice or you tell\nthem you won’t stand for their behavior. You may even pray for them to change.\nIf they refuse, you may distance yourself from them. You make fewer plans with\nthem, and you are slower to return their phone calls. If things become\nunbearable, you might even sever the relationship altogether.\nBut what if the person you are dissatis\u0000ed with is yourself?\nWhat if the person you do not like is yourself?\nWhat if the person you absolutely hate is you?\nPlease pause here. Inhale deeply and slowly exhale a couple of times. Quietly\nshine the spotlight of attention on your relationship with yourself. Bring your\nattention to moments when you felt a beautiful state of care and respect for\nyourself. Breathe deeply and stay in a state of observation of those moments.\nNow bring your attention to moments when you were in suffering states of\ndissatisfaction or dislike for yourself. Breathe deeply and observe your state in\nthose moments.\nHopefully, you have had some moments when you felt a beautiful\nconnection to yourself. You simply loved yourself as you are.\nThere have likely also been times when you felt pain or discomfort within. In\nsuch moments you may have either sought out external solutions and escapes or\naccepted the inner war as “normal,” forgetting that you are not this warring self;\nyou are a beautiful self. You are not a sheep fearing your survival in the jungle;\nyou are a lion.\nIf we don’t have a beautiful relationship with ourselves, everything about us\n—th e way we walk, the way we talk, our speech, our way of thinking, and our\ne\u0000orts at success—will be tainted with a nagging sense of self-doubt. What can\nwe possibly achieve when we are stuck in this su\u0000ering state? We need to dissolve\nit.\nUnable to cope with the ever-widening internal divide, many people\ndesperately try to chisel and re-chisel their bodies. Millions take to narcotics,\ndrink alcohol, or even contemplate or commit suicide.\nAnd yet, for all our grasping at external solutions to changing how we feel\nabout ourselves, there is no evidence that any human being has ever found\nhappiness while at war with oneself. After all, if you spend the majority of your\ntime in this warring state, how much energy will you have left to enjoy your\nrelationships, wealth, leisure, or success?\nWhat else happens when we are not at peace with ourselves?\nDid you ever play tag as a child? The way I played the game was to stand in a\ncircle and say, “Eeny meeny miney mo, catch a tiger by the toe . . . ,” then\neliminate one kid after the other until the last one left was the chaser.\nWhat were we doing? Instead of making a clear choice, we allowed luck to\ndecide. No one needed to take responsibility for the selection of the chaser.\nIf we are not harmonious within ourselves, quite often that is how we\napproach the most important decisions of our lives, even as adults. We are\nindecisive because our su\u0000ering states take away faith and respect for ourselves\nand our decisions. We continue to play “eeny meeny miney mo” in the process of\nselecting our jobs, our spouses, or our business partners. We simply cannot\ncon\u0000dently decide or choose.\nEven if we do choose, we continue to doubt. We doubt that we are in love\nwith the right person, sometimes even after three years of being in a relationship.\nWe doubt our choice of career a decade into the job. We doubt our course of\nstudy in college long after graduation. When we are lost in the clash of\ncon\u0000icting views and opinions, we forget that life can be beautiful indeed.\nUnable to bear this inner chaos, we run from one quick \u0000x to the next,\nlooking for anything to silence the con\u0000icting and nagging voices that create this\ninner claustrophobia. And when nothing leads to any lasting change, it can seem\nas if the world has given us a raw deal.\n“But I’m a good person!” we cry. “I have never harmed anyone in my life. So\nwhy am I so unhappy?”\nThree Expressions of the Warring Self\nIn the famous epic Indian poem Ramayana, the villain, King Ravana, has a\nunique dilemma. He was not a foolish or evil king, like so many of the villains\nwe’ve come to know. Ravana was a great scholar. He was well versed in the\nscriptures, and he created great prosperity for his kingdom.\nSo how did the actions of this man, considered virtuous in every way, lead to\nthe death of his brother, his son, and his entire clan? What led him to kidnap the\nwife of the hero, Rama—an action that led to Ravana’s entire kingdom going\ndown in \u0000ames? How did such an erudite man become so destructive?\nThe story describes Ravana as a man with ten heads. His many heads were\nsymbolic of his dissenting values and obsessive desires that kept him trapped in\nhis own head. None of his knowledge was any help when it came to silencing the\ntorment of his con\u0000icting desires and values. This was a man at war with\nhimself, and it did not take long for that inner war to infect everyone around\nhim.\nWhen you read the story of Ravana, you might \u0000nd yourself asking a\nquestion that is as relevant today as it was in ancient times:\nWhy do good people become bad?\nHave we not all asked this question at some point in our lives? We look at a\nsister, son, or friend who has lost their way and we wonder: What on earth went\nwrong? We look at the leader or the artist we once believed in and ask ourselves:\nHow did they go so far oﬀ track?\nWhen a person is lost in a state of inner war, they become like Ravana. Not\nonly are they self-destructive, but they have the power to destroy others. A\nperson could be the most well-intentioned in a crowd of hundreds, but if their\ninner self is a battleground of con\u0000icting values, they will bring chaos to their\nworld.\nThe \u0000re of our inner war can be sparked by con\u0000icting desires, such as:\nI want to be a selﬂess mother, but then I’ll have to give up my career. . . . I can’t\nhave it all.\nI want that promotion, but then I’ll never be able to travel the world. . . . I guess\nI have to settle.\nI want a relationship, but I don’t want to miss out on the single life. . . . I’m\ngoing to be unhappy no matter what.\nOur inner battle could also be because of the con\u0000ict between our ideals and\nour actualities. We yearn to be virtuous, but are drawn to vice. We yearn to be\npatient and kind, but are \u0000lled with anger and intolerance.\nBut when you have no way of freeing yourself from this kind of inner\ncon\u0000ict, it does not matter which path you take. Your dissatisfaction can\nintensify into states of depression and even a hatred of yourself and your world.\nThat is exactly what happened to Ravana. Even though he knew his\ncon\u0000icting desires would be his undoing, and the fate of his kingdom hung in\nthe balance, he could not resist.\nSo many of us live our lives with the same inner torment as Ravana. But if\nour inner world is a battleground, how can we ever know the beautiful states of\nhappiness or freedom?\n“Battleground” might seem like a strong word. Sure, we all feel unsatis\u0000ed\nand underappreciated a lot of the time. But that’s life.\nOr is it?\nWhile the source of the struggles in our lives may appear to be external, in\nfact it is we who are unleashing a destructive force into the world by embracing\none of the following expressions of the warring self:\nThe \u0000rst expression of our inner war is a “shrinking self.”\nThe Shrinking Self\nWhen Alex was around twelve or thirteen years old, he was bullied because he\nwas frail and much smaller than his classmates. To cope with his state of\nhumiliation, he went on to become a gymnast. By the time he reached college, he\nwas one of the most handsome students on campus: girls virtually swooned over\nhim.\nAnd his success did not stop there. He made it big in business, became\nwealthy, and married a beautiful woman.\nYet, to this day, Alex feels highly self-conscious and inadequate because he\ncan’t resist the desire to compare himself with others. He often feels unsure if his\nwife feels or needs his love because he compares her behavior with that of\nwomen from his past.\nHe had thought that if he rose to the very top in every \u0000eld he pursued, he\nwould have no reason to feel inferior to anyone else. He had decided as a child\nthat he would get to a place where others would compare themselves with him—\ninstead of the other way around.\nBut after embarking on an inner journey of truth, he saw that reality was very\ndi\u0000erent. His obsessive habit of frequent comparison had not ceased despite all\nthe success and wealth he had created!\nThis entanglement in su\u0000ering states led to a strange pattern in his business.\nEvery deal he made, even if it resulted in a good pro\u0000t, wasn’t good enough. He\nbelieved that others always fared better. He also felt that success came to him\nonly after immense hard work. Everyone else, in his mind, had a much easier\nride.\nThis pattern changed only when his inner disharmony dissolved. A new\nintelligence arose in him during a mystic meditation with Krishnaji, in which he\nwoke up from the illusion of a limited self and experienced a state of\nconsciousness in which he no longer felt division or the need to compare.\nAfter Alex experienced that transformation in consciousness, he found it\nmuch easier to access beautiful states of creativity and a clear mind—and it’s\nfrom this place that he now builds his achievements.\nThis shrinking self that Alex experienced is not uncommon. Oftentimes it\nmanifests as debilitating self-consciousness, di\u0000dence, or low self-esteem.\nSometimes, as in the case of Alex, our desperate attempts at enlarging our\nshrinking self can make us extremely aggressive.\nBut what is the truth of the shrinking self?\nThe shrinking self is fueled by the addictive habit of comparing ourselves to\nothers and feeling that we do not match up.\nWe feel small and insigni\u0000cant. We are ill at ease in the presence of those who\nwe believe are more intelligent, more beautiful, and more talented. We imagine\nothers judge us as inferior, and we become self-conscious.\nA shrinking self creates uncertainty and makes us step back from the joys of\nlife. We lack the courage to go after what our heart yearns for.\nThe Destructive Self\nThe second expression of our inner war is a “destructive self.”\nAlicia and Greg, a couple from Switzerland, had been having problems for\nover a decade, but they stayed together for their only daughter. Finally, when\ntheir daughter went away to college, they decided to go ahead with a divorce.\nUnfortunately, their problems did not end there.\nGreg had always been a reasonable man, but once the divorce was \u0000nalized,\nhe changed. He gave in to hate. He made it his mission to make Alicia’s life\ndi\u0000cult. In the years they were married, she had been loud and dominant, while\nhe had been submissive. It was as if the separation had freed him up to indulge in\nyears of pent-up anger and aggression. He constantly compared his disturbing\nand broken family life with how it could have been and blamed his wife for his\nunhappiness.\nGreg was well educated and \u0000nancially independent—he had the means to\nlive a peaceful life—but instead he became obsessed with settling scores.\nWhen we give in to the state of the destructive self, we become emotionally\nimbalanced, impulsive, and volatile. A destructive self can manifest as\nperfectionism, excessive ambition, and ruthlessness—or as an addiction to\npleasure, unwholesome habits, or work.\nFrom this state, we perceive others to be competitors or enemies. Dominating\nthem or displaying our power becomes more important to us than our own\ngrowth and well-being. We make adversaries out of friends and family. We\nbecome hardened and insensitive—and we \u0000nd very few people to call our own.\nIt makes us form unhealthy relationships.\nDriving the destructive self is the addictive habit of comparing our lives with\nwhat is and what should have been.\nBut not only do we compare—w e also hold someone else responsible for our\nvexing reality.\nLife becomes a war.\nThe Inert Self\nWe call the third expression of our inner war an “inert self.”\nFor her entire life, Beth compared herself with her siblings, and she always\ncame up short. She was considered less attractive than her sisters, and a learning\ndisability prevented her from achieving the same professional success they\nenjoyed. Making matters worse, her parents taunted her for being lazy, which\nonly aggravated her inner war.\nAnd so she fell into a vicious cycle: she ate more, she did not exercise or work,\nand she lost the little con\u0000dence she’d once had. She squandered whatever\nmoney she had by making foolish investments. She fantasized about the life she\nknew she deserved but that seemed hopelessly out of reach.\nThe predominant characteristics of an inert self are indi\u0000erence, lack of\nresponsibility, laziness, and procrastination. We lack drive and motivation. The\none activity that tempts us is daydreaming! What drives the experience of the\ninert self? Once again, it’s the habit of comparing ourselves with others—but\nwhereas the shrinking self struggles with comparison and experiences life as a\nkind of endless race, the inert self gives up without e\u0000ort.\nWe give up hope that anything good will come our way.\nIf we happen to \u0000nd ourselves in any of these stories, we may be tempted to\ninstantly write them o\u0000 as bad habits or behavioral traits that need to be \u0000xed.\nIn yearning to break free of the shrinking self, we may get into adventure\nsports. We assume that the solution to low self-esteem is to project a glorious\nself-image—but that only unconsciously furthers our inner divide.\nIn an attempt to transcend the destructive self, we try to re\u0000ne our behavior,\nour culture, or our language. We try to manage and tame ourselves, but often\nend up merely trading in one addiction for another.\nFinally, to break free of the state of inertia, we make desperate e\u0000orts to drag\nourselves to the gym, to detox our bodies, or even cleanse our livers, without ever\naddressing our consciousness.\nAre these lasting solutions? How could there be true transformation without\n\u0000rst bringing attention to the truth that these are but symptoms of a deeper\ninternal war?\nPlease pause here. Breathe deeply. Observe moments of dislike, dissatisfaction, or\nhate for yourself. What self has your inner battle morphed into?\nPassively observe the impact of these selves on your life.\nSearching for Lost Love\nThe great Mughal emperor Akbar was known for indulging in intellectually\nstimulating challenges and debates with his ministers. The story goes that he\nonce threw a rather funny challenge at his ministers:\nHe asked them to \u0000nd the biggest fool in the kingdom.\nBirbal, a particularly witty minister, spent all day searching high and low but\ncame up empty-handed when it came to producing this great fool. By dusk, a\nfatigued Birbal was making his return to the emperor, when his eyes fell on a\nman searching for something under a dim lamppost.\nBirbal approached the man and asked what he was looking for.\nAt the sight of the minister in royal robes, the old man respectfully said that\nhe was searching for his lost key. Filled with sympathy, Birbal joined the man in\nhis search. After a few minutes Birbal asked the man where exactly he had lost\nthe key. The man pointed toward a dark corner very far away from the spot\nwhere he had been searching.\n“If you lost the key over there, why are you searching for it here?”\n“Because here there is light.”\nBirbal smiled, con\u0000dent he had completed the task successfully. He went\nback to the emperor and presented the fool and won his reward.\nHow often do we wonder why none of our external solutions bring peace to\nthe war raging within? Sure, the world abounds with quick \u0000xes that bring on\ntemporary “feel-good” states. But when the next challenge hits, we are\ncatapulted back into the same whirl of con\u0000ict, condemnation, and self-\nloathing.\nLike the old man in Birbal’s story, we cannot solve our problems, because we\ndon’t know where to look for the solutions. Even when we are willing to look\nwithin, we seek to cut through our su\u0000ering states with a sword. We blame\nourselves for our reserve, our de\u0000ated self-worth, or our self-doubt. But what if\nthey are mere manifestations of something deeper?\nAt the root of all unhappiness is an incessant obsession with the self.\nWhen we share this profound and life-transforming realization with people,\nwe are often met with immediate resistance.\nBut it is my partner who cheated . . .\nIt’s my children who don’t listen . . .\nIt’s my boss who takes credit for my work . . .\nSurely there must be exceptions to the idea that “all unhappiness comes from\nself-obsession,” we may think—especially since we are the ones reading a book\non transforming our lives! The one who stays late when others leave \u0000ve minutes\nearly. The one who makes sure the kids are fed, the plumber is paid, the dentist\nappointments are made—and on and on and on . . .\nHow could anyone who works so hard to be a good person possibly be\ncausing their own stress? Surely there is some mistake.\nHow could anyone so selﬂess be self-obsessed?\nFirst of all, let us clarify that there is a clear di\u0000erentiation between sel\u0000shness\nand self-obsession. We are not talking about acting without regard for others.\nWhat we mean by self-obsession is an internal preoccupation with self.\nNow, before we deny our own self-obsession, let us ask ourselves: How often\ndo we wage imaginary wars in our minds, measuring ourselves against others and\nobsessing about what they think about us? How many times have we posted\nsomething on Facebook, imagined a negative reaction, and posted a reactive\nstatement before anyone actually responded?\nAnd how many times have we simply blamed someone else for the way we are\nfeeling, even though it is our own thoughts spinning round and round in a cycle\nof self-obsession?\nLet us not forget that this state of self-preoccupation is the breeding ground\nof all unhappiness and inner war.\nUntil we make peace with ourselves, our inner worlds will be battlegrounds\nof con\u0000ict:\nWhy is that person more loved than I am?\nWhy can’t my life be more like hers?\nHe is so attractive and witty. Why not me?\nWhy was I not born with a silver spoon in my mouth? Why him?\nYou would continue to sing, Why me? Or, Why not me?\nOur inner struggle, however, has nothing to do with external situations. That\nyou are not as tall as your father or as successful as your college roommate—\nthese are facts of life, neither good nor bad. For many people on this\nearth, life is neither easy nor gentle.\nBut when we toss a maelstrom of self-obsession on top of life’s hardships, we\nbecome \u0000xated on the unfairness of life. Our mind makes everything—our\nbodies, our lives, our world—appear incomplete or ugly. We grow discontented with who we are\nand become obsessed with an image of who we should be.
